When a question mentions QKD and 5G/6G together, anchor your answer on three technical touchpoints: (1) ETSI GS QKD 014 / 004 interfaces between QKDC and KMF; (2) 3GPP network slice instance (NSI) with URLLC characteristics (latency < 10 ms, reliability 99.999%); (3) hybrid key derivation (QKD + PQC) per NIST SP 800-56B rev. 2. Mentioning the trusted-node vs MDI-QKD trade-off and the satellite QKD path for beyond-fibre distances earns distinction marks.

Biotechnology Regulation for RNA Interference Therapeutics and Lipid Nanoparticle Delivery Systems

The regulatory pathway for RNA interference (RNAi) therapeutics in India operates at the intersection of the Central Drugs Standard Control Organisation (CDSCO) biologics framework, the Department of Biotechnology (DBT) recombinant DNA safety guidelines, and the Genetic Engineering Appraisal Committee (GEAC) environmental risk assessment for genetically modified organisms (GMOs) when the active pharmaceutical ingredient (API) involves synthetic small interfering RNA (siRNA) or microRNA (miRNA) mimics. The landmark global approval of patisiran (Onpattro) in 2018 — a transthyretin-mediated amyloidosis therapy using siRNA encapsulated in a proprietary lipid nanoparticle (LNP) — informed the precedent for Class C (high-risk) medical device classification of the LNP delivery system co-packaged with the oligonucleotide drug substance. CDSCO's 2021 guidance on oligonucleotide therapeutics specifies requirements for: (a) comprehensive chemistry, manufacturing, and controls (CMC) documentation for the siRNA API including solid-phase synthesis purity, stereochemical control at phosphorothioate linkages, and 2'-O-methyl / 2'-fluoro modification patterns affecting nuclease resistance and immunogenicity; (b) LNP characterisation covering lipid molar ratios (ionizable cationic lipid, helper phospholipid, cholesterol, PEG-lipid), particle size distribution (polydispersity index < 0.2), encapsulation efficiency > 90 %, and residual solvent limits per ICH Q3C; (c) in vitro off-target transcriptome profiling using RNA-seq with differential expression analysis (|log2FC| > 1, FDR < 0.05) across relevant human cell lines; (d) immunogenicity assessment of both siRNA sequence motifs (TLR3, TLR7, TLR8, RIG-I activation) and LNP components (anti-PEG antibody induction, complement activation); and (e) biodistribution and persistence studies in rodent and non-human primate models using quantitative PCR for siRNA strand quantification in liver, spleen, kidney, and brain. The DBT's 2022 guidelines for nucleic acid therapeutics require GEAC clearance for environmental release of LNP-siRNA waste from manufacturing facilities, treating encapsulated siRNA as a GMO derivative until degradation is demonstrated per OECD 301 ready biodegradability tests. Clinical trial design follows ICMR's 2023 consensus statement: Phase I dose-escalation with mandatory sentinel cohort (n=3 per dose level) and 28-day safety observation before expansion; Phase II/III adaptive designs with futility boundaries based on target mRNA knockdown in surrogate tissue (e.g., serum transthyretin for TTR amyloidosis). Post-marketing surveillance leverages the Pharmacovigilance Programme of India (PvPI) with mandatory periodic safety update reports (PSURs) every six months for the first two years. Indian developers, including a CSIR-Institute of Genomics and Integrative Biology (IGIB) consortium and a private biotech firm incubated at C-CAMP, are advancing IND-enabling packages for siRNA targeting hepatitis B virus (HBV) surface antigen and siRNA against PCSK9 for hypercholesterolaemia, with Phase I trials anticipated in 2025–2026 contingent on LNP process scale-up to 200 L GMP batches.

Digital Public Infrastructure for Judicial Case Management: Interoperable Courts Platform and Electronic Filing Standards

The e-Courts Mission Mode Project, now in its Phase III (2023–2027) under the Supreme Court's e-Committee and the Department of Justice, transitions from court-level automation to a federated Interoperable Courts Platform (ICP) that enables seamless case transfer, unified cause lists, and cross-jurisdictional analytics across the Supreme Court, 25 High Courts, and 18,000+ district courts. The ICP architecture rests on three standardisation pillars: (a) the Case Information System (CIS) 3.0 data model, which extends the National Core Version 1.1 schema with JSON-LD semantic annotations for party entities, advocate identifiers (linked to Bar Council of India enrolment numbers), act/section tags mapped to the Indian Kanoon taxonomy, and temporal event streams (filing, listing, order, judgment) with cryptographic hashes for tamper evidence; (b) the Electronic Filing (e-Filing) 2.0 specification specifying PDF/A-3b containers with embedded XML metadata (e-Filing Schema v2.1), digital signatures per the Information Technology Act, 2000 Second Schedule certificates issued by licensed CAs, and mandatory OCR text layer for vernacular language documents using the Bhashini OCR API; and (c) the Unified Case Number (UCN) 2.0 format — a 22-character alphanumeric code encoding court hierarchy, case type, year, sequential number, and a Luhn check digit — replacing the heterogeneous legacy numbering schemes. Interoperability with the Interoperable Criminal Justice System (ICJS) is achieved through a dedicated API gateway exposing REST endpoints for FIR-to-charge-sheet linkage, bail order propagation, and conviction data exchange, secured via mutual TLS with MeitY-approved cipher suites. The National Judicial Data Grid (NJDG) 2.0 serves as the read-only analytics layer, publishing aggregated pendency, disposal, and delay metrics with district-level drill-down, while enforcing differential privacy (with ε = 0.5) for sensitive case categories (POCSO, NDPS, matrimonial). A critical governance challenge is the federated data sovereignty model: each High Court retains administrative control over its CIS instance, with the e-Committee exercising policy authority through the CIS Core Committee. The 2024–2025 milestones include: (a) migration of all High Courts to CIS 3.0 with zero-downtime cutover using database replication and dual-write patterns; (b) deployment of the e-Filing 2.0 mobile application with Aadhaar-based e-KYC for litigant authentication and DigiLocker integration for document fetch; (c) a pilot of AI-assisted case scheduling using a fine-tuned multilingual BERT model on historical listing data to optimise judge-wise board allocation, subject to the Supreme Court's 2023 algorithmic transparency directive requiring model cards and bias audit reports. Budget outlay for Phase III stands at ₹7,210 crore, with 60:40 Centre-State sharing for district court infrastructure and 100% Central funding for High Court and Supreme Court components.

AI Model Governance for Artificial Intelligence Generated Code in Critical Software Supply Chains

The proliferation of large language models (LLMs) for code generation — including Codex, CodeLlama, StarCoder, and indigenous initiatives such as BharatGPT-Code — introduces novel supply-chain risks for safety-critical and national-security software deployed in defence avionics, nuclear instrumentation, railway signalling, and banking core systems. The Ministry of Electronics and Information Technology (MeitY), in coordination with the Office of the National Cyber Security Coordinator (NCSC) and CERT-In, released the "Secure AI-Generated Code Framework" (SAICF) in March 2024, outlining a four-layer governance stack for any AI-generated code incorporated into Critical Information Infrastructure (CII) as defined under the Information Technology Act, 2000 Section 70. Layer 1 — Provenance and Licensing: every AI-generated code snippet must be traced to its training data provenance using Software Bill of Materials (SBOM) in SPDX 2.3 format, with license compatibility analysis (GPL, LGPL, Apache-2.0, BSD) automated via the FOSSology engine. Snippets matching copyleft-licensed training data beyond a defined token-overlap threshold trigger mandatory human review and re-implementation. Layer 2 — Static and Dynamic Analysis: AI-generated code must pass a hardened CI/CD pipeline integrating Semgrep rulesets for CWE Top 25 coverage, CodeQL queries for taint propagation, and fuzzing harnesses (libFuzzer, AFL++) achieving a high branch coverage threshold (e.g., ≥ 85 %). Layer 3 — Supply-Chain Levels for Software Artifacts (SLSA) Level 3 Build Attestation: the build process producing the final binary must generate SLSA provenance attestations signed by a hardware security module (HSM) rooted in the National PKI, with hermetic build environment verification. Layer 4 — Runtime Behavioural Monitoring: deployed binaries derived from AI-generated code are instrumented with eBPF-based syscall tracing and control-flow integrity (CFI) checks, feeding anomaly scores to the National Critical Information Infrastructure Protection Centre (NCIIPC) security operations centre. The SAICF also establishes a "Model Card for Code Generation" registry maintained by the Centre for Development of Advanced Computing (C-DAC), requiring model developers to disclose: (a) training data deduplication and PII scrubbing methodology; (b) vulnerability injection rate measured on the CyberSecEval 2 benchmark; (c) hallucination rate for API signatures measured against the HumanEval+ and MBPP+ test suites; and (d) indemnification clauses for downstream users. For open-source foundation models fine-tuned on proprietary codebases, the framework mandates differential privacy (with ε = 2.0) during fine-tuning to mitigate memorisation leakage. The first compliance audit cycle targets 31 March 2025 for all CII entities, with non-compliance attracting directions under Section 70B of the IT Act.

  • Confusing GaN-on-Si epitaxy readiness with GaN-on-SiC or bulk GaN substrate availability; the former is advancing towards pilot production at SCL, the latter remains import-dependent for high-power RF.
  • Assuming QKD key rates scale linearly with fibre distance; secret key rate decays exponentially with loss (≈ 0.2 dB/km at 1550 nm), making trusted nodes or quantum repeaters necessary beyond 100–150 km.
  • Treating RNAi therapeutics as conventional small molecules; they are regulated as biologics with LNP delivery systems classified as Class C medical devices requiring separate CMC and device master files.
  • Believing the e-Courts ICP is a centralised database; it is a federated platform with High Court-level data sovereignty and standardised APIs, not a monolithic repository.
  • Overlooking that AI-generated code licensing risk arises from training data memorisation, not the model output licence; copyleft contamination requires proactive detection via SBOM and token-overlap thresholds.
  • Mixing up SLSA build levels; Level 3 requires hermetic builds and non-falsifiable provenance, whereas Level 1 only requires automated build scripts.
  • Assuming differential privacy during fine-tuning eliminates all memorisation risk; ε = 2.0 provides bounded but non-zero leakage probability, necessitating post-training auditing.
